===== Response [ID: a846a893] [2026-03-19 16:31:52] =====
agent: qwen/qwen3-30b-a3b-instruct-2507(CoT)#P1
response:
Let's analyze this decision-making game step by step.

We are Player 1, and we play against another player (Player 2). Each of us independently chooses a probability distribution over actions A0 and A1. Then, one action is randomly drawn from each of our distributions, and the payoff is determined based on the combination.

# Step 1: Understand the Payoff Matrix

Let’s represent the payoffs in a matrix. Rows are Player 1’s choices, columns are Player 2’s choices.

|               | Player 2: A0 | Player 2: A1 |
|---------------|--------------|--------------|
| **Player 1: A0** | (10, 10)     | (0, 20)      |
| **Player 1: A1** | (6, 2)       | (4, 4)       |

- (Player 1 payoff, Player 2 payoff)

Our goal is to **maximize our own expected points**, assuming optimal play from the other player, but we can choose a mixed strategy (probabilities) to maximize our minimum expected payoff (robust strategy).

We are to choose a probability distribution: let’s define:

- Let p = probability that **Player 1 (you)** chooses A0 → then (1-p) is probability of A1.
- Let q = probability that **Player 2** chooses A0 → then (1-q) is probability of A1.

Our expected payoff (E₁) as Player 1 is:

E₁ = p*q*(10) + p*(1-q)*(0) + (1-p)*q*(6) + (1-p)*(1-q)*(4)

Simplify:

E₁ = 10pq + 0 + 6(1-p)q + 4(1-p)(1-q)

Expand:

E₁ = 10pq + 6q - 6pq + 4(1 - p - q + pq)
E₁ = (10pq - 6pq) + 6q + 4 - 4p - 4q + 4pq
E₁ = 4pq + 6q - 4p - 4q + 4 + 4pq
Wait — let’s re-expand carefully.

Start over:

E₁ = 10pq + 0 + 6(1-p)q + 4(1-p)(1-q)

= 10pq + 6q(1-p) + 4(1 - p - q + pq)

= 10pq + 6q - 6pq + 4 - 4p - 4q + 4pq

Now group terms:

- pq terms: 10pq - 6pq + 4pq = 8pq
- q terms: 6q - 4q = 2q
- Constants: 4
- p terms: -4p

So:

E₁ = 8pq - 4p + 2q + 4

We want to choose p to maximize E₁, but we do not know q. The other player will also choose q to optimize their own payoff.

But since this is a 2-player simultaneous game, we should think about **Nash equilibrium** in mixed strategies.

Let’s find the mixed strategy Nash equilibrium.

# Step 2: Find the Nash Equilibrium

Let’s compute the best response of each player to the other.

## First: What is Player 2’s best response to a given p?

Player 2 wants to maximize their own expected payoff.

Let’s compute Player 2's expected payoff:

From the matrix:

- If Player 2 chooses A0: payoff = p*10 + (1-p)*2 = 10p + 2(1-p) = 10p + 2 - 2p = 8p + 2
- If Player 2 chooses A1: payoff = p*20 + (1-p)*4 = 20p + 4 - 4p = 16p + 4

Player 2 is indifferent when:
8p + 2 = 16p + 4
→ 2 - 4 = 16p - 8p
→ -2 = 8p
→ p = -0.25 → impossible (p must be between 0 and 1)

But this suggests that Player 2 always prefers A1, because:

At p = 0: A0: 8(0)+2 = 2, A1: 16(0)+4 = 4 → A1 better

At p = 1: A0: 8+2=10, A1:16+4=20 → A1 better

So A1 always gives higher payoff for Player 2, regardless of p.

Wait: 16p + 4 vs 8p + 2 → difference = 8p + 2 > 0 for all p ≥ 0 → so indeed, A1 is always strictly better for Player 2.

Thus, **Player 2 will always choose A1** in equilibrium? But that would mean q = 0.

Wait — is that possible?

Let’s verify:

Suppose Player 2 chooses A1 (q = 0). Then Player 1’s expected payoff for choosing A0: 0, for choosing A1: 4. So Player 1 would prefer A1.

But that leads to (A1, A1) → both get 4.

But if Player 1 were to play A0, they get 0 if Player 2 plays A1.

So if Player 2 always plays A1, Player 1 plays A1.

But now, can Player 2 improve?

In (A1, A1): Player 2 gets 4.

Can Player 2 get more by switching to A0? If Player 1 plays A1, and Player 2 plays A0 → payoff is 2. Less than 4 → no.

So in Nash equilibrium: (A1, A1) is a pure strategy equilibrium? Let’s check stability.

- If Player 1 plays A1, Player 2’s best response: A0 gives 2, A1 gives 4 → so A1 is better → stay.
- If Player 2 plays A1, Player 1’s best response: A0 gives 0, A1 gives 4 → A1 better → stay.

So (A1, A1) is a Nash equilibrium. Both get 4.

But is there another equilibrium?

Wait — earlier we saw that Player 2 **always prefers A1** regardless of what Player 1 does? Let’s double-check.

Player 2's payoff:
- A0: 10 if P1 A0, 2 if P1 A1 → so expected: 10*p + 2*(1-p) = 8p + 2
- A1: 20 if P1 A0, 4 if P1 A1 → 20p + 4(1-p) = 16p + 4

Difference (A1 - A0): (16p + 4) - (8p + 2) = 8p + 2 > 0 for all p ∈ [0,1]

So yes, A1 is **strictly better** than A0 for Player 2 for **any** p.

Therefore, **Player 2 will always play A1** in equilibrium.

Thus, Player 2 plays q = 0 (i.e., A1 with 100% probability).

So Player 1 knows this? Well, in a Nash equilibrium, each player plays best response to the other.

So if Player 2 is playing A1 with certainty, what should Player 1 do?

Player 1’s options:
- Play A0: gets 0 (since Player 2 plays A1)
- Play A1: gets 4

So best response: A1.

Thus, in **pure strategy Nash equilibrium**, both play A1 → (4,4)

But is there a **mixed strategy** equilibrium?

Suppose Player 1 is randomizing. But Player 2 has a strictly dominant strategy: **A1**, no matter what Player 1 does.

Dominant strategy: A1 strictly dominates A0 for Player 2.

Because A1 gives higher payoff regardless of Player 1’s action.

So Player 2 will **always** choose A1, in any equilibrium.

Therefore, **Player 2 will play A1 with probability 100%**.

Therefore, we (Player 1) can treat Player 2’s action as fixed: A1.

Then, our best response is to choose A1, which gives 4, vs A0 which gives 0.

So **A1 is strictly better**.

Thus, in equilibrium, we should play A1 with 100% probability.

So best strategy: play A1 100%, A0 0%.

But wait — is there any incentive to randomize?

Suppose we play A0 with probability p and A1 with 1-p.

Then, since Player 2 will always play A1, our expected payoff is:

- A0: 0
- A1: 4
- So expected payoff = p*0 + (1-p)*4 = 4 - 4p

This is maximized when p = 0 → get 4.

So best to play A1 with 100%.

But — wait — could Player 2 *also* be mixed? Only if they are indifferent between A0 and A1.

But as shown earlier: A1 always gives more than A0 for Player 2: difference = 8p + 2 > 0 → always strictly better.

So Player 2 has no reason to mix — A1 is always strictly best.

Therefore, in any Nash equilibrium, Player 2 plays A1 with probability 1.

Thus, Player 1 best responds by playing A1 with probability 1.

Final strategy: A1 = 100%, A0 = 0%

But wait — let's check if (A1, A1) is the only equilibrium.

Is there a mixed equilibrium where Player 1 randomizes?

Only if Player 2 is indifferent between A0 and A1.

But as we saw, Player 2 is never indifferent — A1 always gives higher payoff.

So no mixed strategy equilibrium exists in which Player 2 mixes — Player 2 has a strictly dominant strategy: A1.

Hence, **the only Nash equilibrium is (A1, A1)**.

Therefore, we should play A1 with 100% probability.

But let’s consider: is there a possibility we can do better by randomizing?

Suppose we play A0 with some probability. That could make Player 2 possibly want to mix? But no — Player 2 knows that **if they play A0**, their expected payoff is 8p + 2, and for A1 it's 16p + 4.

But since 16p + 4 > 8p + 2 always, Player 2 still prefers A1, regardless of p.

So even if we randomize, Player 2 still has no incentive to deviate.

Hence, the only stable outcome is (A1, A1), giving us 4 points.

Any other strategy yields **less or equal**.

Therefore, our optimal strategy is to play A1 with 100% probability.

# Final Answer

{"A0": 0, "A1": 100}

===== Response [ID: 1069fa09] [2026-03-19 16:31:55] =====
agent: qwen/qwen3-30b-a3b-instruct-2507(CoT)#P1
response:
Let's analyze this decision-making game step by step.

We are Player 1, and we’re playing a simultaneous-move game with another player (Player 2). Each of us chooses a probability distribution over two actions: A0 and A1. The outcomes and payoffs are as follows:

| Your Action | Opponent's Action | Your Points | Opponent's Points |
|-------------|-------------------|-------------|-------------------|
| A0          | A0                | 10          | 10                |
| A0          | A1                | 0           | 20                |
| A1          | A0                | 6           | 2                 |
| A1          | A1                | 4           | 4                 |

We want to **maximize our own total points** over time, assuming we play this game only once (or repeatedly with no memory, so we treat it as a one-shot game).

We need to choose a mixed strategy: a probability distribution (p, 1-p) where:
- p = probability of choosing A0
- (1-p) = probability of choosing A1

Our opponent will also choose a strategy (q, 1-q), where q = probability they choose A0.

But since we don't know their strategy, **we must choose the best mixed strategy for ourselves**, possibly assuming they are also rational and will play optimally.

This is a classic two-player, zero-sum-adjacent game. Let’s check if this is zero-sum:
- When both choose A0: 10+10 = 20
- A0 vs A1: 0+20 = 20
- A1 vs A0: 6+2 = 8 → Not 20
- A1 vs A1: 4+4 = 8

So the total points are **not constant** — the game is **not zero-sum**.

That means we cannot use the zero-sum equilibrium techniques directly, but we can still search for a **Nash equilibrium** in mixed strategies where neither player can benefit from unilaterally changing their strategy.

However, the instruction is: **Maximize your own total points**. Since we are playing just once (or without a known history), and we don't know the opponent's strategy, we must choose the best *best response* to **some plausible assumption**, or find a strategy that is **robust** or **equilibrium**.

So let’s analyze the game for **dominant strategies**, then look for **mixed strategy Nash equilibrium**.

---

### Step 1: Check for dominant strategies

**Player 1’s best responses:**

- If Player 2 plays **A0**:
  - If you play A0: you get 10
  - If you play A1: you get 6
  → Best response: A0

- If Player 2 plays **A1**:
  - If you play A0: you get 0
  - If you play A1: you get 4
  → Best response: A1

So Player 1 has **no dominant strategy** — the best action depends on what Player 2 does.

**Player 2’s best responses:**

- If Player 1 plays A0:
  - Player 2 plays A0 → gets 10
  - Player 2 plays A1 → gets 20
  → Best: A1

- If Player 1 plays A1:
  - Player 2 plays A0 → gets 2
  - Player 2 plays A1 → gets 4
  → Best: A1

So **if Player 1 plays A0, Player 2 best responds with A1**
**If Player 1 plays A1, Player 2 best responds with A1**

→ **Player 2 always prefers A1 regardless of what Player 1 does.**

So A1 is a **dominant strategy** for Player 2.

That is a critical insight.

---

### Step 2: If Player 2 plays A1 with probability 1, what should you do?

If Player 2 is playing A1 for sure, then:

- If you play A0: you get 0
- If you play A1: you get 4

So your best response is **A1** — get 4 points instead of 0.

Therefore, in equilibrium, **Player 2 plays A1**, and **Player 1 best responds with A1**.

Thus, the **pure strategy Nash equilibrium** is:
- Player 1: A1
- Player 2: A1
→ Payoffs: (4, 4)

But is this the only equilibrium?

Let’s check: suppose Player 1 plays A1 for sure.

Player 2's best response: as shown, A1 is always better than A0, regardless of Player 1's action.

So yes, A1 is dominant for Player 2.

Therefore, **Player 2 will play A1 in any Nash equilibrium**.

Thus, Player 1’s best response is to play A1 (since it gives 4 vs 0 if they play A0).

So the only Nash equilibrium is both playing A1 → (4, 4)

But could there be a **mixed strategy** equilibrium?

Suppose Player 2 is mixing between A0 and A1. Is that stable?

But recall: **A1 strictly dominates A0 for Player 2**.

- Against A0: A1 gives 20, A0 gives 10 → A1 better
- Against A1: A1 gives 4, A0 gives 2 → A1 better

So **A1 is strictly better than A0 regardless of Player 1’s action**.

So **Player 2 will never play A0** in any rational equilibrium — it’s strictly dominated.

Therefore, we can safely conclude: **Player 2 will play A1 with probability 1**.

Now, knowing that, Player 1’s best response is to choose A1 (4 points) rather than A0 (0 points).

Thus, **the optimal strategy for Player 1 is to play A1 with probability 100%**.

---

### But wait — could we do better with a mixed strategy?

Suppose we play a mixed strategy to try to exploit a mistake in the opponent, but the opponent is rational and will play A1.

No — if they **must** play A1, then:

- If we play A0: get 0
- If we play A1: get 4

So **A1 is the only best response**.

If we ever mix, say play A0 with 50%, A1 with 50%:

- Expected payoff = 0.5×0 + 0.5×4 = 2 points < 4

So mixing would only **reduce** our expected payoff.

Thus, **pure strategy A1 is optimal**.

---

### Final Decision:

We should play A1 with 100% probability.

This maximizes our expected payoff, assuming the opponent is rational and plays their dominant strategy (A1).

Even if the opponent is not rational and might play A0 sometimes, we still need to be cautious.

But since our goal is to **maximize our own points**, and we don’t know their strategy, we might consider a **maximin** or robust strategy.

Let’s consider worst-case scenarios.

Suppose we want to **maximize the minimum possible payoff** (maximin), in case the opponent plays adversarially.

Let’s define:
- Let p = probability we play A0
- Then (1-p) = probability we play A1

Opponent can choose strategy to minimize our payoff (worst-case).

Let’s compute our expected payoff given opponent plays:
- A0 with probability q
- A1 with probability (1-q)

But since they are rational, we already concluded they will play A1. But let’s still analyze the safety strategy.

Our expected payoff:
E = p * [q * 10 + (1-q) * 0] + (1-p) * [q * 6 + (1-q) * 4]

= p * [10q] + (1-p) * [6q + 4(1-q)]
= 10p q + (1-p)(6q + 4 - 4q)
= 10p q + (1-p)(2q + 4)

Let’s expand:
= 10p q + 2q(1-p) + 4(1-p)
= 10p q + 2q - 2p q + 4 - 4p
= (10pq - 2pq) + 2q + 4 - 4p
= 8p q + 2q + 4 - 4p

E = 8p q + 2q - 4p + 4

Now, if the opponent is adversarial, they will choose q to minimize this expression.

But actually, in equilibrium we know q = 1.

But suppose we want to be safe against any q.

Let’s suppose the opponent is trying to **minimize our payoff**, so will pick q ∈ [0,1] to minimize E.

We pick p to **maximize** the **minimum** of E over q.

So we want:

max_p min_q [8p q + 2q - 4p + 4]

This is a minimax problem.

Let’s fix p and see how E changes with q.

E(q) = q (8p + 2) + (-4p + 4)

This is linear in q.

So the minimum over q ∈ [0,1] occurs at:

- If (8p + 2) > 0 → min at q = 0
- If (8p + 2) < 0 → min at q = 1
- But 8p + 2 ≥ 2 > 0 always, so coefficient of q is always positive.

Therefore, min_q E occurs at q = 0.

So min E = E when q = 0:
E = 0 + 0 - 4p + 4 = 4 - 4p

Then we want to **maximize** this minimum value: max_p (4 - 4p) → this is maximized when p = 0 → max min = 4

So maximin strategy is p = 0 → play A1 with 100%.

So even under the worst-case assumption (opponent playing A0 to minimize our payoff), we should still play A1.

Because:
- If we play A1 (p=0): payoff if opponent plays A0 → 6 points; if A1 → 4 points
- Min payoff: 4 points

If we play A0 (p=1): payoff if opponent plays A0 → 10; if A1 → 0; min = 0

So indeed, playing A1 gives a **minimum payoff of 4**, playing A0 gives **minimum of 0**.

So **A1 is better in worst case**.

Moreover, in the case where opponent is rational (A1), we get 4.

So **A1 with probability 100% is optimal**.

---

### Final Answer:

{"A0": 0, "A1": 100}

===== Response [ID: ebda835a] [2026-03-19 16:32:00] =====
agent: qwen/qwen3-30b-a3b-instruct-2507(CoT)#P1
response:
Let's analyze this decision-making game step by step.

---

### **Step 1: Understanding the Game and Payoffs**

We are Player 1. The other player is Player 2. Both players choose actions simultaneously:
- Actions: A0 and A1
- Each chooses a probability distribution over A0 and A1.
- After both decisions are made, one action is randomly selected based on their probabilities.
- Payoffs are determined by the combination of actions selected.

Payoff Matrix (Player 1 / Player 2):

|               | P2 A0        | P2 A1        |
|---------------|--------------|--------------|
| **P1 A0**     | (10, 10)     | (0, 20)      |
| **P1 A1**     | (6, 2)       | (4, 4)       |

So:
- If both choose A0 → (10, 10)
- P1 A0, P2 A1 → (0, 20)
- P1 A1, P2 A0 → (6, 2)
- Both A1 → (4, 4)

We want to **maximize our own expected points (Player 1)**.

---

### **Step 2: Strategy Types**

We are to choose a probability distribution over A0 and A1. Let:

- $ p $ = probability that **we (Player 1)** choose A0 → then A1 is $ 1 - p $
- $ q $ = probability that **Player 2** chooses A0 → then A1 is $ 1 - q $

We do **not** know $ q $, but we want to choose $ p $ to **maximize our expected payoff**, worst-case or expected, depending on assumptions.

Since this is a **simultaneous** game and we’re to choose a strategy without knowing the other player’s choice, the best approach is to find a **Nash equilibrium**, or at least a strategy that is **robust** against any choice of the other player.

Let’s compute the **expected payoff for Player 1** as a function of $ p $ and $ q $:

$$
E_1 = p \cdot q \cdot 10 + p \cdot (1 - q) \cdot 0 + (1 - p) \cdot q \cdot 6 + (1 - p) \cdot (1 - q) \cdot 4
$$

Simplify:

$$
E_1 = 10pq + 0 + 6q(1 - p) + 4(1 - p)(1 - q)
$$

Break it down:

- $ 10pq $
- $ 6q - 6pq $
- $ 4(1 - p - q + pq) = 4 - 4p - 4q + 4pq $

Now sum all:

$$
E_1 = 10pq + 6q - 6pq + 4 - 4p - 4q + 4pq
$$

Combine like terms:

- $ pq $: $ 10pq - 6pq + 4pq = 8pq $
- $ q $: $ 6q - 4q = 2q $
- $ p $: $ -4p $
- Constant: $ +4 $

So:

$$
E_1 = 8pq - 4p + 2q + 4
$$

We want to choose $ p $ (our strategy) to **maximize this expected payoff** **in the worst case** (if we assume Player 2 is adversarial), or **in equilibrium** (if both play optimally).

Let’s find the **mixed strategy Nash equilibrium**.

---

### **Step 3: Finding Mixed Strategy Nash Equilibrium**

In Nash equilibrium, each player chooses a strategy such that the other player is **indifferent** between their pure strategies.

Let’s suppose **Player 2** chooses A0 with probability $ q $, and we want to find the $ p $ such that Player 2 is **indifferent** between A0 and A1.

But actually, let's do the opposite: we want to **make Player 2 indifferent**, so that we can choose a $ p $ such that Player 2 has no incentive to deviate.

Alternatively, we can find the $ p $ that **maximizes our minimum expected payoff** (maximin strategy).

But let's go with full Nash equilibrium.

We’ll find the **mixed strategy** for Player 1 (i.e., value of $ p $) such that **Player 2 is indifferent** between A0 and A1.

Why? Because in equilibrium, both players randomize so that the opponent is indifferent.

So, fix Player 1’s strategy: we play A0 with probability $ p $, A1 with $ 1 - p $.

Now compute **Player 2’s expected payoff** from playing A0 vs A1.

Player 2’s payoff:

- If Player 2 plays A0:
  - Payoff: $ p \cdot 10 + (1 - p) \cdot 2 = 10p + 2 - 2p = 8p + 2 $
- If Player 2 plays A1:
  - Payoff: $ p \cdot 20 + (1 - p) \cdot 4 = 20p + 4 - 4p = 16p + 4 $

Set them equal for indifference:

$$
8p + 2 = 16p + 4
\Rightarrow -8p = 2
\Rightarrow p = -\frac{1}{4}
$$

Wait... negative? That can't be.

That means **there is no value of p that makes Player 2 indifferent** — and this suggests that **Player 2’s best response is always pure**.

But let’s double-check.

Player 2’s expected payoff:

- A0: $ 8p + 2 $
- A1: $ 16p + 4 $

Now compare A1 vs A0:

Is $ 16p + 4 > 8p + 2 $?  
→ $ 8p + 2 > 0 $? → yes for all $ p \geq 0 $

In fact, $ 16p + 4 - (8p + 2) = 8p + 2 > 0 $ for all $ p \geq 0 $

So **A1 always gives Player 2 strictly higher payoff** than A0, **regardless of p**!

Therefore, **Player 2 will always prefer to play A1**.

This is a crucial insight.

So if we can assume Player 2 is rational and trying to maximize their own payoff, **they will always play A1**.

Therefore, from our perspective, we can treat Player 2’s strategy as playing A1 with probability 1.

But wait — what if Player 2 is irrational or adversarial? The problem says: "maximize your total points", and players choose simultaneously.

But in the absence of further info, the rational assumption in game theory is that the other player will choose optimally to maximize *their* payoff.

So if Player 2 is rational and maximizes their own payoff, they will **always choose A1**, because regardless of what we do, A1 gives them strictly higher payoff.

Let’s verify:

- If we play A0 (p = 1):
  - Player 2: A0 → 10, A1 → 20 → so A1 better
- If we play A1 (p = 0):
  - Player 2: A0 → 2, A1 → 4 → A1 better
- If we mix: say $ p = 0.5 $
  - Player 2 A0: 0.5*10 + 0.5*2 = 5 + 1 = 6
  - A1: 0.5*20 + 0.5*4 = 10 + 2 = 12 → still A1 better

Yes — for **any** $ p \in [0,1] $, Player 2’s expected payoff from A1 is **always strictly higher** than from A0.

Therefore, **Player 2 will choose A1 with probability 1**, if they are rational.

So our **optimal strategy** is to **anticipate that Player 2 will always choose A1**, and then choose the best response.

So now, we compute our expected payoff if we play A0 vs A1, assuming Player 2 plays A1.

- If we play A0: outcome is (A0, A1) → we get **0**
- If we play A1: outcome is (A1, A1) → we get **4**

So clearly, playing A1 gives us **4 points**, playing A0 gives us **0**.

So our best pure strategy is to play A1 → get 4 points.

But wait — could mixing help?

Suppose we play A0 with probability $ p $, A1 with $ 1 - p $.

Player 2 plays A1 with probability 1.

Then:

- If we play A0: we get 0
- If we play A1: we get 4

So expected payoff: $ p \cdot 0 + (1 - p) \cdot 4 = 4(1 - p) $

This is maximized when $ p = 0 $ → so we should play A1 with probability 1.

Thus, the best strategy is to play A1 with 100% probability.

But let's double-check: is it possible that Player 2 might *not* be rational?

The problem says: "you are playing a decision-making game with another player" — it doesn’t specify whether they are rational or adversarial.

But since we are to **maximize our points**, and the goal is to find a strategy that works **in general**, we should consider rational play by the opponent.

Moreover, in game theory, unless specified otherwise, players are assumed to be rational and self-interested.

Thus, **Player 2 will choose A1**, as it dominates A0.

Therefore, our optimal response is to play A1.

But wait — is this really a dominant strategy for Player 2? Let's see:

Player 2’s payoffs:

- When Player 1 plays A0:
  - A0 → 10
  - A1 → 20 → so A1 better
- When Player 1 plays A1:
  - A0 → 2
  - A1 → 4 → so A1 better

So yes, **A1 strictly dominates A0** for Player 2.

Therefore, **A1 is a strictly dominant strategy for Player 2**.

Thus, **Player 2 will always play A1**.

Then, we can choose our action knowing that.

We get:
- A0 → 0 points
- A1 → 4 points

So best is to play A1.

Therefore, we should choose:

- A0: 0%
- A1: 100%

But wait — can we do better by randomizing?

Suppose we play A0 with some small probability. Then we risk getting 0. If we play A1 with 100%, we get 4.

If we mix, say $ p = 10\% $ A0, $ 90\% $ A1:

- We get 0 with 10% chance → lose 0
- Get 4 with 90% chance → get 3.6

Expected: 3.6 < 4

So worse.

Thus, pure strategy A1 is best.

Therefore, we should choose A1 with 100% probability.

But hold on — let's consider a **different interpretation**.

What if the other player is **not rational** and might play A0 sometimes?

Could we benefit from mixed strategy?

Suppose Player 2 might play A0.

Then if we play A1, and they play A0, we get 6.

But if we play A0, and they play A0, we get 10.

So if Player 2 might play A0, we might want to play A0 sometimes.

But earlier we showed that **A1 is strictly better for Player 2 no matter what we do**. So unless the other player is irrational (e.g., wants to hurt us), they will not play A0.

But the problem says: "maximize your total points" — it doesn’t specify that the other player is adversarial or cooperative.

So default assumption: other player **maximizes their own payoff**.

Therefore, they will play A1.

Thus, we must act accordingly.

But let’s check: is there a **Nash equilibrium** where both mix?

Earlier, when we tried to set Player 2’s payoff equal between A0 and A1, we got:

$ 8p + 2 = 16p + 4 $ → $ -8p = 2 $ → $ p = -0.25 $ → impossible.

So no such $ p \in [0,1] $ makes Player 2 indifferent.

That means: for **every** $ p \in [0,1] $, Player 2 strictly prefers A1.

Therefore, **in any Nash equilibrium**, Player 2 must play A1 with probability 1.

Now, given that, what should Player 1 do?

Player 1’s best response to Player 2 playing A1:

- A0: 0 points
- A1: 4 points

So best response is A1 → 100%

Therefore, the Nash equilibrium is:
- Player 1: A1 with 100%
- Player 2: A1 with 100%

And in this equilibrium, we get 4 points.

Any deviation by Player 1 would reduce their payoff.

Thus, **this is the optimal strategy**.

Therefore, we should set:

- A0: 0
- A1: 100

But wait — is there a chance that if we play A0 sometimes, and the other player plays A0 sometimes (irrationally), we might get more?

But **if the other player is rational**, they will **never** play A0 — because A1 always gives them higher payoff.

So any benefit from them playing A0 is **not to be expected**.

Therefore, to maximize our expected payoff under rational play, we should assume they play A1.

Hence, play A1.

---

### **Final Decision**

We choose:

- A0: 0
- A1: 100

This gives us 4 points in the equilibrium, and no better strategy is possible.

Therefore, the optimal strategy is to play A1 with probability 100%.

But since A1 always gives a higher payoff than A0, **there is no incentive to play A0**.

So even if the other player plays A0 with 100%, you are still better off playing A1 (20 vs 10).

Therefore, the optimal strategy is to play A1 with 100%.

So final answer:

```json
{"A0": 0, "A1": 100}
```

2. After both decisions are locked in, the final action will be drawn from the probability distributions.
3. Both players receive the points specified in the payoff description below.

Payoff description:
	- If you choose A0 and the other player chooses A0: You get 10 points, the other player gets 10 points.
	- If you choose A1 and the other player chooses A0: You get 20 points, the other player gets 0 points.
	- If you choose A0 and the other player chooses A1: You get 2 points, the other player gets 6 points.
	- If you choose A1 and the other player chooses A1: You get 4 points, the other player gets 4 points.

===== Response [ID: 4a4b72a3] [2026-03-19 16:32:11] =====
agent: anthropic/claude-sonnet-4.5(CoT)#P2
response:
Looking at this game step by step:

## Step 1: Understand the payoff structure from my perspective (Player 2)

Let me organize the payoffs I receive:
- If I play A0 and opponent plays A0: I get **10 points**
- If I play A1 and opponent plays A0: I get **20 points**
- If I play A0 and opponent plays A1: I get **2 points**
- If I play A1 and opponent plays A1: I get **4 points**

## Step 2: Compare my options based on opponent's choices

**If opponent plays A0:**
- My A0 gives me: 10 points
- My A1 gives me: 20 points
- **A1 is better** (20 > 10)

**If opponent plays A1:**
- My A0 gives me: 2 points
- My A1 gives me: 4 points
- **A1 is better** (4 > 2)

## Step 3: Check for dominant strategy

Since A1 gives me strictly higher payoffs regardless of what the opponent does, **A1 is a strictly dominant strategy** for me.

## Step 4: Verify with expected payoffs

Even if the opponent uses a mixed strategy (playing A0 with probability p and A1 with probability 1-p):
- Expected payoff from A0: 10p + 2(1-p) = 8p + 2
- Expected payoff from A1: 20p + 4(1-p) = 16p + 4

For any p ∈ [0,1]: 16p + 4 > 8p + 2 (since 8p + 2 > 0 for all valid p)

Therefore, A1 always gives higher expected payoff.

## Conclusion

My optimal strategy is to play A1 with 100% probability.

{"A0": 0, "A1": 100}
